Shopify Stock Fell After Its Report. Here's the Price I'd Pay for It.

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

The e-commerce company's free cash flow climbed 61% last quarter, but the stock fell anyway. Is it cheap enough now?

Shopify (SHOP -8.74%) stock fell nearly 9% on Tuesday after the e-commerce software company posted its third-quarter results. At roughly $144 as I write, shares are about 20% below their Oct. 12 record close of $179.22.

The slide came even though revenue climbed 32% year over year to about $3.7 billion and free cash flow jumped 61% to $816 million.

Before the report, I wrote that I'd rather wait and see where shares settled afterward. The stock was around $157 then, or about 86 times its trailing free cash flow. Now the cash flow has come in slightly over the $800 million I'd penciled in for a strong quarter, and the price is lower. Is it finally time to buy?

Investors seemed to want more than a quarter that landed where management said it would. Shopify's guidance was for third-quarter revenue growth in the low 30s, and 32% delivered that.

But revenue had grown 34% in each of the two previous quarters, beating a low-thirties outlook for the first quarter and a high-twenties forecast for the second.

Gross merchandise volume (GMV, the value of orders placed on Shopify's platform) slowed, too. It rose 29% to $119 billion in the third quarter, after gaining 35% in the first quarter and 32% in the second. Currency explains most of that. In constant currency, GMV grew 30% in all three quarters. Gross profit, though, is where the slowdown shows. Its growth rate dipped to 28% in the third quarter, under the 32% and 31% rates in the first two quarters. And management sees high-twenties growth again in the fourth quarter.

Spending is also set to rise. Management guided fourth-quarter operating expenses to 29% to 30% of revenue. In the fourth quarter of 2025, they were about 29%. Chief financial officer Jeff Hoffmeister said on the call that the forecast reflects more marketing, including some efforts before Black Friday and Cyber Monday. A U.S. marketing test held down spending in the third quarter, and he said part of that spending will move to the fourth.

Taking the middle of each range in the guidance, I get fourth-quarter operating income growth in the high teens. That means profit growth might slow sharply from the 66% Shopify just reported.

I doubt any of these explains Tuesday's fall by itself. But together, they suggest profit growth may ease in the quarters ahead.

Shopify's free cash flow margin (the part of revenue left as cash after capital spending) was 22%, against 18% in the third quarter of 2025. This pushed trailing-12-month free cash flow to around $2.7 billion, up from nearly $2.4 billion through June.

Between the higher cash flow and the lower price, the stock's valuation looks much less stretched than it did last week. At a market cap of around $185 billion, Shopify now trades at about 70 times trailing free cash flow, against about 86 times free cash flow before the report and about 98 times free cash flow at the Oct. 12 record.

Still, not all of that improvement is likely to last. Hoffmeister said only about 1.5 percentage points of the margin gain came from lasting cost discipline. He called the rest more temporary, spread about evenly between the marketing test, the timing of some tax receivables, and an accounting change that shifted merchant cash advances (Shopify's financing for its sellers) out of operating cash flow. Management also expects the fourth-quarter free cash flow margin to roughly match last year's 19%.

Taking out the temporary piece, third-quarter free cash flow would have been closer to $725 million. That comes out to around 72 times trailing free cash flow, just slightly above 70.

Yes, the e-commerce company is still growing quickly and bringing in much more cash than it did a year ago. Shopify even spent $562 million of that cash to buy back its own shares in the third quarter.

But at around 70 times free cash flow, buyers are still paying for years of rapid growth. And they're paying it right as gross profit growth cools and management boosts its marketing.

Does the lower price make Shopify a buy? I think the drop moved the stock closer to a price I'd pay, but not all the way there. I'd start to consider shares somewhere around $115, or about 55 times trailing free cash flow.

That's about 20% under where the stock sits now, so I'm in no rush.

Daniel Sparks and his clients do not have positions in any of the stocks mentioned. The Motley Fool has positions in and recommends Shopify. The Motley Fool has a disclosure policy.

*Average returns of all recommendations since inception. Cost basis and return based on previous market day close.

Dirk Kuyt, Liverpool’s big-game hero and embodiment of the Benitez era

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

English fans can be excused for never been quite sure what to expect when a striker swaps the Eredivisie for the Premier League.

Will they be the next Robin van Persie or Ruud van Nistelrooy? Or are they being sold false goods? Think Ricky van Wolfswinkel. A star in Holland with Utrecht and then in Portugal with Sporting but the scorer of precisely one goal in England during a full season with Norwich.

Dirk Kuyt had plundered 71 goals in 101 games for Feyenoord before Liverpool pressed ‘buy’ in the summer of 2006. In theory, the Reds were getting a bargain at just £10million. A proven goalscorer and, those who had seen him in action, the very antithesis of a goal hanger.

Yes, Kuyt ran around a lot. He worked for the team. He put in a shift. Ironically, that was part of the reason why his career at Liverpool will be remembered as that of honest pro rather than superstar.

Kuyt, with those solid legs and neat mop of blonde hair, made his debut in a 2-1 home win over West Ham on August 26, 2006, coming on as a substitute to replace Peter Crouch.

That 2006-07 Liverpool team could be classed as being ‘between strikers’.

Crouch brought something different to the mix and Craig Bellamy was a classy pest with a goal or two in him, while Robbie Fowler had come back for a second stint but was more nostalgia act than genuine first-teamer so there was a goalscoring void to be filled. Was Kuyt the man?

This, of course, was the Rafa Benitez era at Anfield. His tactical wizardry had guided a genuinely mixed bag of a Liverpool team to Champions League glory in 2005, but now there was the Holy Grail of trying to win the Premier League.

Anyone and everyone can chip in on cup runs, but a title assault needs a genuine and regular goalscorer. A 20-goal haul from Kuyt and solid contributions from elsewhere could have been the winning formula.

The Dutchman took a little while to settle but scored twice in four days at the end of September, helping Liverpool to home wins against Newcastle and Spurs.

Reds fans were hopeful but cautious too. On the one hand, there was a feeling that could this be the start of something. On the other, there was a nagging feeling that he was good but not great. A seven out of 10 player.

Fans are greedy. They want superstars. They want No.9s and No.10s. Kuyt looked like what he was. A £10million signing from the Dutch league.

He scored 14 goals in that first campaign, a very respectable haul but not a game-changing one.

In the next, he managed 11 while a late flurry in Liverpool’s near-miss 2008-09 season yielded 15.

It was back to 11 in the following campaign but back up to 15 in 2009-10, a tally boosted by one very special afternoon against Manchester United.

One thing was very clear. Rafa loved him. Or rather, given that the Spaniard doesn’t really do love, Rafa saw how valuable he was to the cause. From being an out-and-out striker, Benitez turned Kuyt into the ultimate workhorse, ploughing a furrow up and down one side of a front three.

READ: When Dirk Kuyt shamelessly stole Luis Suarez’s wondergoal v Man Utd

And Liverpool fans adjusted with him. The fanciful expectations of Kuyt being a world-beater had been downgraded to a different type of admiration. He wasn’t Pele, but good old Dirk would play his heart out for the club and die for the cause. And that goes a long, long way on Merseyside.

While other Reds flickered and flirted with greatness, Kuyt offered a rubber-stamped guarantee. Whatever the occasion, whatever the opponent, whatever the location, he would put in a blue-collar shift week in, week out.

That can sound patronising, but Kuyt had another trick up his sleeve which moved him into a category beyond honest trier. The man from the little coastal town of Katwijk aan See (Dutch for Clacton-on-Sea… well, not really) could score big goals in big games.

When he finally left the Big Anfield House in 2012, his ‘best bits’ were quite something:

– Scorer in the 2006-07 Champions League final against AC Milan

– Scorer of the winning penalty in the 2007 semi-final second leg against Chelsea.

– First player in 21 years to score a hat-trick against Manchester United

– Injury-time penalty to secure dramatic derby win at Everton

– Extra-time goal against Cardiff in the 2012 League Cup final

– Shootout goal against Cardiff to secure League Cup final glory

There were other important strikes against Arsenal, while he netted seven times in the 2007-08 Champions League campaign. Even the hat-trick against Manchester United had added cult value as they were all one-yard tap-ins. Those goalscoring instincts had never left him.

Not many can claim to have scored a hat-trick against Manchester United.

But there was Dirk @Kuyt, #onthisday in 2011… ⚽⚽⚽ pic.twitter.com/TzdKhIi54X

In many ways, Dirk Kuyt was the embodiment of the Benitez era. Tactically astute, hard-working and capable of taking it up a level on big occasions.

You don’t build a football team just by throwing 11 superstars together. Even Brazil 70 had a few in their ranks who provided solidity and balance. That was Dirk Kuyt at Liverpool. Popular, reliable and hugely valuable.

Imagine Kuyt was at his peak now. Would he make this current high-flying Liverpool team? Not as a regular starter, but Jurgen Klopp would have him as a squad player all day long.

Slight injury concern for one of the litany of superstar forwards in the squad? Two games in four days? Tough away assignment where you need to dig in but try to nick a big goal?

Deep ocean oxygen kept rising and falling long after animals appeared

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Geologists found deep ocean oxygen built up and vanished again for over 100 million years after the Cambrian, complicating how animals evolved.

Researchers often explain the Cambrian Explosion, the burst of animal life half a billion years ago, with a lasting rise in ocean oxygen. In the deep ocean, though, the oxygen didn’t last..

Geologists studied mud that settled on the seafloor between about 485 million and 380 million years ago and later hardened into rock.

They found oxygen near the bottom building up for short stretches and then disappearing again. So animals may have evolved under less deep-sea oxygen than researchers once assumed.

Chadlin Ostrander, an assistant professor in the Geology & Geophysics department at the University of Utah, led the study. His team found no evidence of sustained oxygen in the deep ocean during that span.

The team concluded that oxygen didn’t settle into the deepest water for good until sometime after roughly 380 million years ago.

“Just a decade ago, it was thought that a deep ocean oxygenation switch was flipped around 540 million years ago. Our new dataset pushes that forward in time by at least ~160 million years,” he said.

The team didn’t measure oxygen directly. Instead, it read a chemical record in the rock that follows oxygen indirectly.

Researchers have invoked oxygen in the deep ocean to explain nearly every major step in early animal history. That includes the first animals and the Cambrian Explosion. It also includes a second burst of marine diversity from about 485 to 444 million years ago.

Other scientists have raised serious counterarguments to each. A common objection is that oxygen may have risen during these events, but not high enough, or for long enough, to fill the deep ocean.

Visitors to the Grand Canyon can see some of that early history. Its walls include about 1,600 feet (500 meters) of rock that settled in a Cambrian sea.

Ostrander’s samples start right after the Cambrian ended, about 485 million years ago, so the team didn’t sample that sea.

Geologists find tiny amounts of thallium, a heavy metal, in ordinary rock. It comes in two forms that differ by a hair in weight.

When a manganese mineral settles into seafloor mud, it pulls more of the heavier form out of seawater. That mineral survives in mud only where oxygen is steadily present in the water above it, and a few days without oxygen dissolve it again.

Mud formed in oxygen-poor places keeps a sample of the surrounding seawater, including its mix of the two forms. The team read a lower value, meaning more of the lighter form, as a sign that more seafloor lay under oxygen-rich water.

The team measured 82 shale samples from the Peel River in Canada’s Yukon. The river has cut into the rock, exposing fresh layers that stayed largely unweathered in the cold.

Erik Sperling of Stanford University and Justin Strauss of Dartmouth College led the expeditions that collected the Yukon mud.

Another 44 samples came from sites including Latvia, Libya, Wales and Montana.

For most of the record, the team found the same thallium mix as in ordinary continental rock. A seafloor with little oxygen above it should leave exactly that. In Yukon mud that formed between about 405 million and 386 million years ago, the readings dropped.

The lowest value came from a Marcellus Shale sample from the Appalachian Basin that formed about 388 million years ago. By the team’s calculations, that value means oxygen-rich water covered much more than 30% of the seafloor, well past the continental shelves and into the deep ocean.

Readings from the peak of the last ice age look similar, with surface oxygen reaching the deep ocean only slightly less effectively than it does now.

That high-oxygen stretch didn’t last. By about 378 million years ago, the readings returned to ordinary values and stayed there to the top of the rock. That could mean more of the deep ocean had lost its oxygen again.

Local conditions could have faked the dip, so the team checked. The 11 Yukon samples from the dip had the same chemistry as the other Yukon samples, and the Marcellus Shale, from a separate basin, dipped the same way.

By the time of those samples, Ostrander said, marine life was already large and doing energy-expensive work.

“You have some phenomenal changes in evolutionary biology happening during this time. And none of it seems to require substantial and sustained deep ocean oxygenation,” he said.

Animal life was changing fast before that window opened.

Paleontologists working at a fossil site in southwest China have found that many animal groups already existed before the Cambrian began. Researchers who analyzed fossil trails from about 545 million years ago reached a similar conclusion.

In rock about 520 million years old, researchers have found colonies of tiny filter-feeding animals and an early relative of octopuses and squid.

The deep ocean has had steady oxygen since then. The team can’t say when that began, only that it came after about 380 million years ago. Ostrander called that the study’s one disappointment.

Gaps remain. The team has only one rock sample for roughly every 450,000 years, so the real swings may have been even larger than the mud shows.

The team also couldn’t fully rule out that thallium cycled through seawater too fast for the mud to reflect the whole ocean. Its models and the match between basins make that unlikely.

The researchers wrote that long, closely spaced oxygen records from the late Paleozoic and the Mesozoic are still needed to learn when the deep ocean filled with oxygen for good.

The full study was published in the journal Science Advances.

Sign up for the Global Ocean Oxygen Network‘s webinars to follow the science. The UNESCO expert group on falling ocean oxygen runs monthly talks, and its short form is open to anyone.

Donate to the Chesapeake Bay Foundation to support work on low-oxygen dead zones. The foundation’s scientists track the Bay’s summer areas where nutrient pollution leaves too little oxygen for fish and crabs.

Look into GeoCorps America for field experience.  The Geological Society of America places geoscientists on paid, short-term projects on public lands, including paleontology.

Volunteer with the Paleontological Research Institution for hands-on work. Its Museum of the Earth in Ithaca, New York, recruits volunteers to help visitors identify fossils, teach school groups, and prepare specimens.

Join Earth Science Week if you want to share geology with others. The American Geosciences Institute leads the annual event, and community groups, educators, and individuals organize their own activities.

What Trump losing power over Congress means for Iran, Ukraine and foreign policy

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

With Democrats taking control of Congress, U.S. President Donald Trump could intensify his focus on foreign policy in his final two years in office, while facing greater scrutiny over the wars in Iran and Ukraine, experts told CNBC.

The Associated Press is projecting Democrats will have a Senate majority, flipping seats from Republicans in Iowa, Kansas, North Carolina, Ohio, South Carolina, and Texas.

Democrats also won control of the House, giving the party greater scope to scrutinize the administration, shape spending and challenge Trump's foreign-policy agenda.

"We are going to see a president that is somewhat constrained on matters of oversight and investigation, but on foreign policy, he does still retain a lot of latitude," Laurel Rapp, director of Chatham House's U.S. and North America Programme, told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe."

Rapp said the Iran war was a "major issue" in this election cycle amid soaring fuel prices, food costs, and inflation. The war began when Trump launched joint strikes with Israel on Feb. 28.

"Republicans don't really have an off-ramp here at this stage. We know the Democrats will seek to take votes in the House and the Senate to constrain the Republicans, to restrain the administration on Iran war powers. But whether that will bind the administration is another matter," Rapp added.

She said that Democrats could exercise the "power of the purse" to limit spending on U.S. military and other Iran-related costs.

With control of both chambers, Democrats could pass legislation seeking to direct Trump to end U.S. involvement in the conflict. However, making such a measure binding over a presidential veto would require enough votes in Congress to override it.

J.C. Lintzenich, visiting senior fellow at the German Marshall Fund, said that the president is not a "lame duck."

"Remember that this election changes the Congress; it does not change the president," Lintzenich told CNBC's "Squawk Box Europe" on Wednesday.

"The President of the United States still has lots of power, lots of authority. We still have pending action in Iran. We still have an unresolved situation with Russia and Ukraine."

He added that while Congress has changed, foreign policy is still executed by the president and his appointed officials.

"What we'll now see is a little bit more congressional oversight, the application of funding, for example, Ukraine, that's required by statute and law," Lintzenich said.

Lintzenich said the outgoing Congress was "more willing to let the White House slide," but Democratic control of both chambers would mean a more assertive Congress, using its Article I powers over legislation, spending, and oversight to hold the White House more closely to existing law and congressional requirements.

Bruno Fernandes' Heartfelt Interview After Man Utd 3-2 Roma Draws Apology

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

James is a Senior Editor for GIVEMESPORT with over eight years of experience in the industry. After graduating from the University of Southampton in 2016 with a degree in history, he joined the company as an Academy member in July 2017 and, after a short time working as a freelancer, was given a full-time role in February 2018.  He primarily covers the biggest stories in the football world but is also comfortable reporting on a variety of other sports including darts, golf, Formula 1, tennis, boxing and athletics. He has covered some massive events during his time at GIVEMESPORT, including numerous Champions League and World Cup finals, as well as the Olympics in 2020 and 2024.

Bruno Fernandes played a starring role as Manchester United came from two goals down to beat Roma in their Champions League clash on Tuesday evening.

United went into the match on a nine-game winless run, and it looked as if that dismal run of form would continue when the Italian side took a two-goal lead inside the opening 17 minutes.

However, the Red Devils were able to pull off an impressive second-half comeback. Fernandes, who caused controversy when he questioned Michael Carrick's tactics after their defeat to Como last month, halved the deficit from the penalty spot with just over half-an-hour remaining.

Matheus Cunha equalised in the 83rd minute and then scored what turned out to be the winner three minutes later to ensure United picked up all three points in dramatic fashion.

The pressure on Carrick has eased slightly after United's victory. Fernandes was asked about Carrick's future at the club after the match and he spoke from the heart. The 32-year-old told Amazon Prime:

“We don’t need to save anyone’s job. The club needed that win. The players needed that win. It is something we needed as a group of players and staff. We have been working so hard to get out of this situation together."

Fernande then addressed Carrick's decision to drop him following his comments after the defeat to Como. He added:

“It has been tough. There has been a lot of speculation in the media. A lot of lies around many things that have happened at this club, between me and the manager, like that we have cleared the air. Nothing like that happened.

“The manager took a decision of making myself on the bench because I wasn’t performing. And that’s fair. Everyone felt like that was a punishment for the words that I had [after Como]. And if it was, it was right.

“The manager makes the decisions. I have to accept it. I have to go forward with that. It doesn’t change the belief I have in the manager, the staff and every player at this club. And it doesn’t change my aim to win every f***ing game that I have ahead of me.”

Fernandes ended the interview by saying that the result should mark a turning point for the club.

Amazon Prime presenter Gabby Logan apologised to viewers after Fernandes' X-rated comment.

United have now won two and lost two of their opening four Champions League matches of the season. The Red Devils, who are currently 17th in the league phase table, are next in European action when they travel to Portugal to face Sporting CP on November 25.

The 20-time English champions welcome Aston Villa to Old Trafford for their Premier League clash on Sunday.

Nithya Raman and Karen Bass Head to Costner ‘Swing Vote’ Territory

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Wasn’t it only Florida that was supposed to be this dramatic?

They traded barbs for months and pillaged supporters by the barrel. So it was only fitting after all this that challenger Nithya Raman and incumbent Karen Bass would be deadlocked and separated by just a few thousand votes four hours after the polls closed in the L.A. mayor’s race.Hey, when you’ve got a good show going, you don’t cancel it mid-season.So Raman’s TV writer husband Vali Chandrasekaran could tell you. Or so anyone watching this race could tell you.As of midnight in Los Angeles some 4,000 votes separated Bass, in the lead, with Raman just behind her, with 600,000 votes counted. Given that there are 580,000 votes remaining, that lead tells us nothing.And given the fact that counting takes a long time — anything mail-in ballot postmarked today would still count in the next week — don’t expect a result anytime soon. Raman and Bass are both Democrats, but Raman is to the left of Bass, if not as far to the left as her would-be New York counterpart Zohran Mamdani. Both Bass and Raman have pledged to do a lot for the film industry — but if you were running for mayor of Los Angeles right now, would you say anything else?

Still, this race isn’t the closest of the evening. That dubious honor goes to Scott Colom and Cindy Hyde-Smith vying for a Mississippi senate seat. With nearly a million votes in, Colom leads by…28 votes.

Raman has been here before, just last June in fact, when she initially trailed rival Spencer Pratt in the primary for the second spot behind Bass and then slowly clawed her way back to finally take the spot a week later.Bass has been here before too. Her race was similarly tight with Rick Caruso four years ago, and she would go on to win by nearly ten points. It took three weeks.Get ready for a lot of post-credit scenes.

Rotherham United boss Alex Bruce on job talk plus clarification on player absence

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Rotherham's elimination from the EFL Trophy was confirmed following a 1-0 home reverse to Newcastle United under-21s in front of a crowd of just 805.

The only goal of the game arrived on 34 minutes through Magpies player Kyran Thompson, with the north-easterners progressing to the next stage alongside Northern Group C winners Bradford City. The Millers did not muster an effort on target in the second period.

Did you know with a Digital subscription to Yorkshire Post, you can get access to all of our premium content, as well as benefiting from fewer ads, loyalty rewards and much more.

Bruce's side have not win a competitive game since September 19. They visit Bromley in the FA Cup this weekend and are 22nd in League Two.

Asked whether his job is in jeopardy, Bruce said: "It's not about me. If there's a fight to be had, I'll have it, but I know the buck stops with me."

On his side's winless streak extending, he continued: "In large parts of it, there wasn't really that much in the game.

"They counter-attacked us in the first half and scored. We didn't do enough to win the game. I think we huffed and puffed.

"As I've just said to the players in the dressing room, to win football matches, you have to show a little bit more than that. If we are going to get out of the trouble that we're in, we need to start showing a bit more than we did."

Despite being available for the competition, Jili Buyabu - dismissed for elbowing a Swindon Town player in last Saturday's League Two fixture - was not involved, with Bruce revealing he had been ordered to stay away from the training ground following his senseless dismissal.

Bruce continued: "I've not seen him since the Swindon game.

"He's stayed away for a couple of days. I told him to stay away. He's back in on Thursday, so we'll look at things then.

"It doesn't take a rocket scientist to realise why he's not been involved.

"I accept people make mistakes, I don't accept certain other things. When he comes back into the group, he needs to show an appetite to wear the shirt in the right way. On Saturday, he didn't do that."

Trump posted through the U.S. midterm elections, and he wasnt happy

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

President Donald Trump has been posting through the U.S. midterm elections, and he doesn't appear happy with the results.

Millions of Americans went to the polls on Tuesday, casting their vote to choose their members of Congress. Democrats decisively took control of both the Senate and House of Representatives in a sweeping midterm victory, one which featured several upsets. South Carolina elected Annie Andrews as its first Democratic senator in 28 years. Texas voted in James Talarico as its first Democratic senator in over 30 years. Even Kansas swung blue, with Adam Hamilton becoming the first Democrat elected to its Senate seat in 94 years.

Still, it's possible that nobody was more upset than Trump. The U.S. president characteristically took to his social media platform Truth Social on election night, expressing his displeasure with the midterm's results as they came in.

Trump's day started off well, the president posting at 12:07 p.m. ET / 9:07 a.m. PT that Guam had been called as a Republican victory. Guam has elected Republican Tony Ada as governor, taking the position from the Democrats for the first time since 2014.

"Guam, which has always been considered strongly Democrat, just had an early call for a REPUBLICAN Victory in the top spot, Governor — Congratulations to Tony Ada!" he wrote. "Also, the Republicans have the Majority in the State Senate. Wow, hopefully that’s an indication for things to come!"

However, Guam's election was separate from the U.S. midterm elections, and had no impact on the makeup of the Senate or House. Once the midterm results began rolling in, the Republicans' situation began looking less rosy.

At 4:11 p.m. ET / 1:11 p.m. PT, Trump shared a screenshot from right-wing YouTuber Benny Johnson in which he claimed that "Republicans are currently showing signs of PRESIDENTIAL-LEVEL midterms turnout already on Election Day." The president also posted that had been watching the pre-election results on Fox News, sharing a clip of commentators praising him. Republican pollster Matt Towery refuted the New York Times' prediction of a "blue surge" in the video, stating that his own polling placed both parties much more evenly matched.

Despite this, Trump began attacking Fox News by 6:36 p.m. ET / 3:36 p.m. PT, shortly after the first polls started to close.

"The FoxNews Poll, always the worst, is so insulting with the questions they ask. Every question is a hit job, and every answer is 'BULLSHIT!'" wrote the U.S. president.

Trump continued to criticise Fox News less than half an hour later at 7:00 p.m. ET / 4:00 p.m. PT, claiming that its ratings are down "because they’re spending too much time covering the Trump Deranged Dumocrats." Fox News' viewership is down 27 percent compared to the same time last year, with Fox Corp. CEO Lachlan Murdoch blaming "viewer fatigue" after "some incredible news cycle[s] the last [few] years."

"All of those people who used to watch Fox, and no longer do, get it," Trump wrote. "It’s them, not me — My numbers are better than they have ever been."

Trump's approval rating fell to a record low just prior to the midterm elections, a Reuters/Ipsos poll finding that just 31 percent of Americans approve of his performance in office.

"The new CNN Poll stated that 'TRUMP' is at 99% among Republicans," Trump continued, misinterpreting a 2024 poll in which Trump received 99 percent of the "MAGA GOP" vote specifically when put up against Democrat Kamala Harris.

"Among Republican Presidents, 'TRUMP' is at 53%, compared to Ronald Reagan at 18%, and Abraham Lincoln at 8%!," he wrote, citing a CNN poll asking Republicans which president throughout history they most admire. The same poll found that 64 percent of Democrats most admired former president Barack Obama. Without accounting for party affiliation, 30 percent chose Obama, while 19 percent named Trump.

"No wonder FoxNews Ratings are collapsing, and they’re going to go down a lot further," Trump posted, further blasting Fox Corp. for donating to Democratic candidates. Fox Corp. donates to both Republicans and Democrats. "They’re way down, not because of me, but because they are AGAINST me!"

By the time of Trump's next posts at around 10:37 p.m. ET / 7:37 p.m. PT, the midterm election results were clearly indicating a Democratic sweep.

"I wasn’t on the Ballot and if I was, we would have won IN A LANDSLIDE! Thank you for your attention to this matter," Trump wrote on Truth Social. "I'm going to become a Political Pundit, both good and bad, on the reasons these Candidates won and lost!"

The president then posted an image of a red cap with the word "veto" embroidered upon it, reminiscent of his "Make America Great Again" hats. Calling in to Fox News, Trump said he was "a little bit surprised" by the midterm election results, and stated that he intended to deal with the Democratic majority Senate and House by blocking its attempts to pass legislation.

"It's the power of veto, it's one of the stronger powers that a president has. And they'll send all the things that are insane and I'll veto them and after a couple of months of vetos they'll come over and they'll say, 'how do we get this stuff done?' and we'll work together," said Trump. "I have the power of veto. The power of veto is impervious."

The U.S. Constitution does allow presidents to refuse to sign legislation, however it is not impassable. Congress can override a president's veto if it has a two-thirds majority in both the House and Senate.

While Trump called in to speak to Fox News, he also blamed the program for the Republicans candidates' poor results. The U.S. president ended midterm election night by claiming on Truth Social that Fox News "bears responsibility for these losses by constantly and consistently putting on the Dumocrat opposition." Many former Republican voters have reported growing disillusionment with the party since Trump took office for his second term, disapproving of how he has handled issues such as the economy and the war in Iran.

Amanda Yeo is an Assistant Editor at Mashable, covering entertainment, culture, tech, science, and social good. Based in Australia, she writes about everything from video games and K-pop to movies and gadgets.

Syfy Cancelled Its Ambitious Sci-Fi Series — Now It’s Prime Video’s Greatest Space Opera

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

After just three seasons, Syfy decided to pull the plug on an ambitious sci-fi series. Adapted from a bestselling book series, the show in question consistently landed in critics' books and even acquired a cult following. Yet, its run was cut short after three seasons. Fortunately, this is when Prime Video stepped in and renewed the series before it lasted for three more seasons.

Adapted from James S.A. Corey's acclaimed book series, The Expanse was first developed by Mark Fergus and Hawk Ostby for the Syfy Network. However, in 2018, Syfy prematurely ended the show's run in 2018. Sci-fi fans were devastated as The Expanse was easily one of the greatest additions to the genre in the 21st century.

Even some of the best sci-fi series, no matter how much potential they initially show, do not return after being canceled. Perhaps, in a parallel world, The Expanse, too, saw the same fate. Fate, however, had different plans for the series. After extensive campaigns from viewers, Amazon picked up the show and continued it for three more seasons, which cemented its "masterpiece" status.

Even in its first three seasons, The Expanse was undeniably an incredible sci-fi series. This explains why many relentlessly campaigned to bring it back. However, it was still short of becoming the masterpiece it eventually turned into. Everything from its portrayal of Newtonian physics to geopolitical conflicts between Earth, Mars, and the Belt were compelling in the first three seasons.

However, the show initially struggled with pacing issues. Especially in season 1, The Expanse felt inconsistent when it balanced Joe Miller's detective noir arc on Ceres with overarching political tension surrounding the Rocinante crew's escape. Syfy's network budget also limited the visual scope of the series, although its VFX team still did an incredible job.

Even main characters in The Expanse, like Holden, Amos, Naomi, and Alex, were yet to break free from their early archetypes in the show's initial chapters. The Expanse season 3 also had to race through Book 2 (Caliban's War) and significantly compress Book 3 (Abaddon's Gate) before ending its run. All in all, the sci-fi series was from its true potential after three installments.

The Expanse started putting its brilliance on full display after it returned for three more seasons on Prime Video. If it wasn't for its revival, it seems possible that The Expanse would have become another forgotten sci-fi series that was canceled a little too soon.

Landing on Prime Video after its revival not only allowed The Expanse to slightly extend its episode runtimes but also become more realistic. With no broadcast censors holding the show back from exploring the grit and violence of its world, The Expanse gradually became a more accurate and relatable representation of how space colonization works.

Going into season 5, The Expanse also finally got to split the Rocinante crew across different locations. This, in turn, helped the series explore and test the individual strengths and journey of its main roster. Even story beats surrounding the Ring Gate and the Marco Inaros and the Free Navy appeared much later, allowing the series to dive deeper into its portrayal of revolutionary stress and economic extinction.

All in all, if Prime Video had not given The Expanse a second chance, its legacy would have been far less long-lasting than it is today. While it is unfortunate that even Prime Video canceled the show before it could cover the complete length of its source material, it is hard not to be grateful that Syfy's cancellation did not mark the end of its run.

The Expanse is a sci-fi series set in the distant future where humanity has spread out across the solar system, but the alliances between the three most potent governing bodies have reached a state of cold war. A mixed-up crew finds themselves at the center of a dangerous intergalactic conspiracy that threatens to bring war to the colonized worlds.

We want to hear from you. Share your perspective in the comments below, and please keep the conversation respectful.

Local media hail QPR star who ’caused problems’ for Burnley

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

QPR drew 1-1 with the Clarets at home in their latest Championship outing and fought back from 1-0. The visitors took the lead in the first-half through Largie Ramazani before the Hoops equalised after the interval via Chair.

The R’s have slipped down to 11th in the table after 14 fixtures but are still within touching distance of the play-offs. They are only one point behind West Ham in 8th as they look to rise back into the top eight over the coming weeks.

Chair was a stand out performer for the London outfit in their most recent outing and local media outlet West London Sport have delivered their verdict on his display: “Missed a great chance when teed up by Kolli straight after coming on, but his trademark composure and slickness in the final third was immediately back on display. Caused problems for Burnley coming in from the left and set up Varane’s goal. Chair’s introduction ultimately turned the game back towards the R’s.”

QPR are back in action on Saturday with a home clash against Lincoln City as they aim to return to winning ways. Their upcoming opponents were promoted from League One earlier this year.

Chair has been a great servant to QPR over recent times. The Belgium-born man has been on their books since joining them back in 2017.

The ex-Morocco international, who made 12 caps, has made eight appearances in all competitions for the Hoops so far this season, seven of which have come in the league. He has chipped in with two goals and two assists.

Chair, who was born in Antwerp, has played just under 300 games for the Hoops and should hit that milestone at some point later in this campaign. He gives Julien Stephan more quality in his attacking department.

He battles it out with the likes of Harvey Vale, Koki Saito and Kwame Poku, among others, for a place in QPR’s starting XI. If he can stay injury free for the rest of this term, there is no reason why he can’t rack up some impressive numbers.

QPR are aiming to be in and around the promotion picture this term. Chair is a proven performer at second tier level and is likely to play a big role over the coming weeks and months, with Lincoln City up next.

He was linked with an exit over the summer to Wrexham but nothing materialised in the end. The Hoops will be pleased that he stayed put beyond the last deadline in September and will be hoping for the same again in January.

Winter Container Inspiration From New Jersey

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Hi GPOD! Today we’re returning to New Jersey to see more from Ellen Goldfarb and Tracy Buttrick. After sharing their beautiful fall containers with us, Ellen is back with some winter inspiration using colorful branches, evergreens, berries, pinecones, and more.

This beautiful antique lead planter in Ridgewood, New Jersey, always calls for a generous amount of greenery and nice height. We try to change what we do from year to year. This time we used curly willow, artificial white berry picks, magnolia leaves, red winterberry (Ilex verticillata, Zones 3–9), and frosted pinecones. Silver bell eucalyptus, silver brunia stems, noble fir (Abies procera, Zones 5–6), and incense cedar (Calocedrus decurrens, Zones 5–8) fill out the arrangement.

This is the same antique planter and house as in the previous photo, but from another winter. That year we used a combination of yellow twig dogwood (Cornus sericea ‘Flaviramea’, Zones 3–7) and red twig dogwood (Cornus sericea cvs., Zones 2–7) for lots of winter color. We also used artificial berry picks in pink and white and natural brown pine cones.

We like to use real berries, but sometimes the birds eat them before the season ends. Artificial berries give the client guaranteed color for longer. For greenery, we used desert juniper (Juniperus osteosperma, Zones 3–9), balsam fir (Abies balsamea, Zones 3–6), and incense cedar.

This slim planter sits on a small sliver of a step at an entry in Ho-Ho-Kus, New Jersey. It is simple but one of my favorites, with stunning red twig dogwood stems. We buy these from a farm that also grows and sells the more common darker-red stems.

Beneath the red is a froth of desert juniper. Dried lotus pods (Nelumbo cvs., Zones 4–10) and clusters of silver brunia punctuate the greenery. The finishing touch is a frilly skirt of bright chartreuse incense cedar.

This one sounds like an outfit, doesn’t it?

One of a pair of pots on the front porch of a Mission-style house in Teaneck, New Jersey, these had plenty of space for tall branches. We liked the combination of golden curly willow (Salix matsudana ‘Tortuosa’, Zones 4–8) and redtwig dogwood against the creamy tan stucco.

We tucked in shiny magnolia leaves with taller silver bell eucalyptus, then added natural brown pinecones and small clumps of silver brunia. A generous, draping swath of incense cedar finishes the bottom of the arrangement.

This is a window box in Englewood, New Jersey. A classic house calls for a classic planting. We used red winterberries, magnolia leaves, frosted pinecones, and silver brunia clusters. Some noble fir and our favorite evergreen, incense cedar, finish the arrangement.

This is a close-up detail of a planter in Franklin Lakes, New Jersey. Dark red twig dogwood stems are mixed with curly willow and branches of multiflora rose hips. We love using the rose hips, but they are sometimes hard to find.

This pot also had artificial white berry picks and Eastern white pine (Pinus strobus, Zones 3–8). Silver brunia on longer stems, noble fir, and incense cedar complete the arrangement.

Thank you for sharing more of your beautiful container designs with us, Ellen and Tracy! I love seeing how many different looks can come from a relatively small palette of winter materials. Changing the color and shape of the branches, berries, cones, and evergreens gives each container its own personality.

We have even more of Ellen and Tracy’s winter containers to share, so we’ll be back tomorrow with another round of inspiration!

Choke of the century? Chengdu collapse sets up thrilling Chinese Super League climax

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

John Aloisi’s side need a point on the final day to seal their maiden title – but their form has dropped off a cliff

With four games of the Chinese Super League to go, Chengdu Rongcheng needed just a point to clinch their first title. With one game to go, they still need a point. What was a formality has become a formidable task. The biggest bottle job in Asian football history is very much on and it would be as popular as it would be dramatic.

The coach of the Sichuan club, the former Portsmouth and Coventry striker John Aloisi, has seen a 12-point lead over Dalian Yingbo cut to just three before Sunday’s showdowns. Whatever happens, this is a positive and thrilling end to a season that started negatively – literally – with nine of the 16 top-tier clubs kicking off on minus points. They had been punished as part of a “special campaign to crack down on match-fixing, gambling and corruption in the football industry”, said a joint statement from the Chinese Football Association, the Ministry of Public Security and the General Administration of Sport at the time.

Chengdu were one of the seven to start on zero, above the defending champions, Shanghai Port, who lost five points and the runners-up, Shanghai Shenhua, who were hit with 10. It robbed the usual favourites of energy and it was all a bit surreal.

It almost felt over as soon as it started with Chengdu opening up a big lead, dropping just two points from the first 12 games while the usual powers struggled to get going.

Chengdu is a major city more known for its fiery Sichuan cuisine, genteel temples and cute pandas than football, despite English attempts to change that. In 2006, Sheffield United bought a local club but the Chengdu Blades were eventually dissolved in 2015. Four years later, City Football Group bought a stake in Sichuan Jiuniu but then moved the club to the southern city of Shenzhen in 2024.

Chengdu Rongcheng were formed in 2018, coming up through the divisions to finally, this season, be within touching distance of the championship. But just two wins from the last 13 and three straight defeats mean the leaders are not limping to the line but have collapsed right next to it.

It should have been over weeks ago. On 10 October, more than 40,000 fans were ready to celebrate the title but an added-time goal from Alberto Quiles gave Tianjin Jinmen Tiger a 1-0 win and postponed the celebrations. Next up was a trip to Shanghai Port but that ended in a 3-0 defeat. The penultimate game of the season came against challengers Dalian Yingbo, when again, just avoiding defeat was needed. It was another painful experience as they lost 1-0 after missing a first-half penalty and having a 90th-minute equaliser ruled out by the video assistant referee.

Chengdu have not scored in almost seven hours of football while Dalian are the form team with four consecutive wins. If they win a fifth against Tianjin then Chengdu need a point at fourth-placed Shandong Taishan as the league is decided by head-to-head results before goal difference. Not many think they will. Even fewer hope so. Dalian and the 60,000 fans that attend home games are the people’s choice. Chengdu are not popular because their fans have developed a poor reputation. Last November, the club was fined about $70,000 (£52,200) after some home supporters racially abused Henan FC’s Ghanaian striker Frank Acheampong, just weeks after a punishment of $3,000 after travelling supporters at a Champions League game in South Korea threw bottles at the Ulsan HD players.

Whatever happens it is an unexpected and dramatic ending to a season that started in shock and the usual negative international headlines that surround Chinese football. The grandstand finish is welcome and average attendances have risen. They are set to finish at about 30,000, a considerable jump from last season’s 25,755 and, by some distance, the best in Asia. Regional leagues have been enjoying some popularity too, showing that there is genuine love for the game in the country; a passion that grows better when it is organic from the bottom rather than organised from the top.

Kick off your evenings with the Guardian's take on the world of football

The national side is still a problem, though, and even for a fanbase well accustomed to disappointment the recent 5-0 defeat to Palestine at home, the fourth-biggest loss in history, was tough to take. As one journalist said, being thrashed by Brazil, Japan and Wales is one thing, but Palestine was another. There were some mitigating circumstances in that a number of national team players were at the Asian Games, where China actually performed well, losing narrowly to old nemesis South Korea in the bronze final.

There are signs that the gap behind Korea is not as wide as it used to be. China reached the final of the under-17 and under-23 Asian championships this year before losing both against Japan. Compared with the rest of Asia’s best, young Chinese players are closing the gap at a technical and tactical level. Exporting more players to Europe is necessary to play at a higher level and if that happens, that senior national shirt may feel a little lighter.

Failure can, however, be hard to shake off as Chengdu Rongcheng may well find out if they mess this up.

Has a footballer ever run a marathon faster than James Milner? | The Knowledge

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Plus: managers sacked after signing new deals, war victims commemorated on kits and the first club website (part II)

Mail us with all of your questions and answers

It came as no surprise to the Knowledge that James Milner had spent the early months of his retirement training for – and then running in – the New York City Marathon. Yes, the bleep test-loving former Brighton, Liverpool and Manchester City player recorded an excellent time of 3hr 9min 4sec, an average of 7min 13sec per mile. Not bad for a 40-year-old whose physique is more like that of a wrestler than a whippet. “A few years ago, I feel like I could have definitely done sub-3:00,” he said, lamenting his late-career injuries for hindering him.

In 2012 we ran a column listing footballers’ fastest marathon times, with the former Leicester playmaker Muzzy Izzet leading the pack on 3hr 22min 36sec at the 2011 London Marathon. That very respectable time has now been surpassed by the Premier League’s record appearance maker, so we thought we had better take a gulp of our isotonic energy drink and run through a few more tales of football’s marathon men and women.

More recent marathon attempts by either active or former footballers puts Izzet down in 12th place, just behind André Schürrle, who clocked 3hr 21min 25sec in Berlin last year, though clearly wanted a greater challenge than a mere 26.2 miles. This year, the 35-year-old completed the 167.7-mile six-stage Marathon des Sables in 51hr 41min 16sec, placing 675th overall. He also climbed Mont Blanc. But back to marathons, where the fastest time we can find for a female footballer is 3hr 40min 30sec by the former USA midfielder Lauren Holiday. She is closely followed by her good friends from the USWNT, Leslie Osborne (3hr 41min 33sec) and Abby Wambach (3hr 44min 25sec).

The Fulham manager, Álvaro Arbeloa, is 11th on our overall list (3hr 15min 21sec, Valencia 2019), with Kevin Kilbane, who always looked like he had a marathon runner’s spring in his step, 10th on 3hr 14min 44sec (London, 2013). A tip of the hat now to Danny Mills, father to the GB Olympic runner George Mills, with Mills Sr running the 2023 London Marathon in 3hr 12min 18sec at 45 years of age. Meanwhile, Milner’s run places him eighth as we approach some of football’s more serious runners.

The former Dundee United and West Ham defender Christian Dailly clocked a seriously impressive 3hr 6min 26sec at the 2013 London Marathon. “In 1990, I went from boys’ football in May to my Dundee United debut in August,” said Dailly after the race. “We’d be down at the beach, and all of a sudden, I’d be running through the sea at St Andrews or up and down the sand dunes. When I got to pre-season at Dundee United, I was flying, running away from everyone.” Dailly went into athletics coaching when his football career ended. We did not expect a goalkeepeer to be jostling for position towards the front of the pack, but the former Derby County and Sheffield Wednesday stopper Lee Grant hared around London in 3hr 0min 33sec this year.

And so we get to the hallowed ground of a sub-three hour marathon and a select group of only five. The Real Madrid legend Raúl González was clearly being cheered from the rafters as he ran his home marathon in 2hr 59min 22sec in 2019. “It was only the second marathon of my life. I wanted to break three hours, and I’m very happy with what I’ve accomplished,” he said. The Oxford United manager, Aaron Ramsey, used his relative youth at 35 to tear up the Stockholm Marathon this summer in exactly 2hr 59min. Arjen Robben was known for his blistering pace over short distances in his glittering career but he showed that he could blend speed with stamina as he raced round Rotterdam in 2023 in a time of 2hr 58min 33sec.

Before setting out on his managerial career, Luis Enrique trained like a demon for the Florence Marathon, with his hard work paying off as he came home in 2hr 58min 8sec. He has since continued his fitness regime, competing in the Frankfurt Ironman event and the Marathon des Sables.

But the gold medal for the fastest former footballer to run a marathon goes to Alexei Smertin. The former Chelsea and Russia utility man took endurance running seriously when he retired in 2008 and hit a peak of 2hr 48min 17sec at the Kazan Marathon in 2020, aged 45. That is an incredible pace of 6min 25sec per mile. He has since tried switching to longer distances as he has aged but believes he may still have one or two quick marathons left in him. “I’ve been leaning toward ultras. One reason is that speed declines with age,” he said recently. “I thought I’d never be able to improve my personal best [2:48:17] … [but] I have a few more years to improve my speed.”

“What is the soonest a manager has been sacked after signing a contract extension?” asks Dave Smythe.

Adam Clark comes in hot with the first suggestion: “Leam Richardson, then of Wigan Athletic, signed a three-year contract extension on 25 October 2022 and was sacked 16 days later on 10 November 2022. Wigan then recruited Kolo Touré on a three-and-a-half year contract, who subsequently lasted just nine games.”

“I’m pretty sure when Watford sacked Glenn Roeder in 1996 he’d signed a contract extension about a fortnight earlier?” suggests Daz Pearce. Good shout, Daz: Watford were not yet in their era of sacking a manager every month, but Roeder – initially appointed at Vicarage Road in 1993 – was given the boot by Watford just 10 days after signing a new one-year contract extension in February 1996. “I am sure I have learned from this experience,” said Roeder, who announced his intention to stay in the game. He would not get another managerial job for another five years, enjoying a successful stint at West Ham from 2001-03.

Not a contract extension but Kevin Cullis’s appointment at Swansea City in 1996 lasted just seven days. Cullis, who never played professionally, had only ever previously coached non-league Crawley Town’s youth team and was later jailed for fraud in 2003, and didn’t get off to the best start in Wales with Swans players reportedly ignoring his tactics at half-time against Blackpool during just his second game in charge. Cullis resigned shortly afterwards.

Elsewhere, Dave Bassett lasted less than four days at Crystal Palace in 1984, Marcelo Bielsa resigned as manager of Lazio after just two days in the job in 2016 and Leroy Rosenior’s 10-minute stint at Torquay United is Knowledge folklore. You can read more about that here.

We have received some new responses to last week’s Knowledge archive, which features some 2016 answers to the question: who were the first football club to have an official website?

“Last week, Martyn Amos reported an Ipswich fan website started in 1990,” wrote a sceptical Alan Terlep (and others), “but history records that the first known website went online in August 1991, so if that’s correct he has a claim to fame well beyond the Knowledge. A fan site that was already active in 1993 may well be the oldest or there though.”

Fellow reader Mark Francome, who works at a global IT service provider so should know a thing or two about this, emails that “Luton Town fans had ‘WHOSH’ (‘Worldwide Hatters On The Super Highway’), which dates from 1994,” while “Millwall also had ‘You’ll Never Make The Station’ from around the same time. No records exist to confirm the internet rumour that Millwall admins hacked their Luton counterparts and ripped up the HTML.”

“Portsmouth’s new kit has names of fallen soldiers on it. Are there other examples of clubs recognising war victims on their kit?” asked Simon Cook in August 2014.

Andrew Walker points us towards Raith Rovers’ away kit for the 2014-15 season, which has been designed to pay tribute to the 16th Royal Scots regiment, a battalion formed of sportsmen, seven of whom came from the Kirkcaldy club. Three Rovers players – Jimmy Todd, Jimmy Scott and George McLay – were killed during the conflict.

“We wanted to do something completely different for next season in terms of kit colours,” the Raith chair, Turnbull Hutton, told the Dundee Courier,” but at the same time the board has been considering a suitable way to mark this centenary in an appropriate manner, so we have brought these two ambitions together with our new green and black strip. “We hope that it captures the imagination of the Scottish footballing public and, through next season’s sports pages, that it ensures that we really do remember the sacrifices of our players and so many others 100 years ago.”

And in Wales, Aberystwyth Town are also commemorating the 100th anniversary of the outbreak of the first world war. “We’re the kit suppliers to Aberystwyth Town Football Club in the Welsh Premier League,” write the good folk at RM Teamwear, “and this season we produced their home strip honouring the centenary of the first world war, more specifically on the sleeves we have the names of players who played for the club and lost their lives in the Great War. Their name, rank and regiment are on players 1-14 in the war veteran player’s position he used to play. Others have “In Memory of Players of This Club Who Fell in the Great War” as seen in the attached photo.”

“Not only were Rangers v Dundee United and Manchester City v Barnsley in 2004-05 the only British league matches to finish 7-1 that season, the same opposing goalkeepers also played in both matches,” flags Stephen Toal. “Ronald Waterreus played for Rangers and Manchester City while Nick Colgan turned out for both Dundee United and Barnsley. Are there any other examples of opposing goalkeepers coming up against each other twice in the same season for a different set of opposing teams?”

“Following Coventry City’s 3-1 victory over Sunderland, I noticed a potentially unique statistic involving Luke O’Nien, who has now played in defeats against Coventry in all four divisions of English league football. Is this a first?” ponders Kirk Savage.

“Has ever been a headed goal awarded goal of the season?” wonders Rupert Sheard. “I can think of a few belters, but awards tend to go to ones kicked in.”

“Which club team has had the most international captains playing together at one time?” asks MH Zahid. “Conversely, which international team has had the most club captains in its starting lineup at one time?”

West Ham took too long to sack Nuno and now must trust Parker to do what he does best | Jacob Steinberg

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Forget speculation over how a Parker-led side would fare in the Premier League and let the man who has taken three clubs up do his work again

It has taken West Ham six months to come to their senses and sack Nuno Espírito Santo, but at least the decision has arrived early enough to revive their hopes of automatic promotion.

Dithering was no longer an option after the dismal performance against West Brom last Saturday.

West Ham, eighth in the Championship and four points off the top two, had to act after a second consecutive home defeat. They are in danger of slipping into a battle for a playoff spot and needed to move on from Nuno after just over a year of his confused, passive football.

Nuno did a terrible job after replacing Graham Potter as manager in September 2025. The 52-year-old had 33 games to save West Ham from relegation and failed. He has the most expensive squad in the Championship but his side have not lived up to their billing as title favourites and their stuttering form has exposed the folly of not sacking Nuno at the end of last season.

David Sullivan, then the club’s largest shareholder, wanted a change. But Daniel Kretinsky favoured continuity and looked to capitalise on the shifting power dynamics in the boardroom. Karren Brady had stepped down as vice-chair in April – Karim Virani replaced her in an interim role – and there was further upheaval when Sullivan resigned as joint-chair and a director of the club to fight what he described as “false allegations” regarding his personal conduct.

Sullivan bears most culpability for West Ham’s relegation. He still owns a 40% stake but is no longer on the board and West Ham are better off without him dictating key decisions. Equally, though, where Sullivan was not mistaken was in wanting Nuno gone. Kretinsky got his first big call wrong. At least the Czech billionaire has given Nuno’s replacement time to get the team back on track. The task falls to Scott Parker, who has been on the market since leaving Burnley last April. Parker is regarded as a “safe” appointment and has been on West Ham’s radar for a while.

Some wanted the former England midfielder in the summer and his name was mentioned again when West Ham failed to win their first three league games. Patience was wearing thin. A subsequent run of four wins in a row swiftly proved a false dawn.

West Ham have not had the right mindset. They have two wins from their past six games and have often started playing only after going a goal down. A 6-0 win against Wrexham was a highlight but victory at Lincoln by the same scoreline came with the caveat that the Imps received two red cards before half-time.

There was no identity under Nuno. West Ham are unbeaten on the road and won 1-0 at the then table-topping Swansea last month but it has been hard to tell Nuno broke the Football League transfer record with the £22m signing of the Belgium midfielder Arne Engels from Celtic. Even Jarrod Bowen, who was convinced to sign a new deal in the summer, has been off colour. Nuno’s tactics mean the winger, West Ham’s top scorer, is often crowded out.

West Ham were lopsided against West Brom. Enough was enough. Kretinsky, who is not at every game, was unimpressed when he attended West Ham’s 3-2 defeat by Southampton a week earlier. He pays attention to the views of Bowen, Tomas Soucek and Mark Noble, the technical director and former club captain.

Nuno, whose substitutions were almost as weird as his starting lineups, had lost the players. The mood darkened after the West Brom game. West Ham were easy to play through because of Nuno’s reluctance to use a holding midfielder, an issue exacerbated by his refusal to use Edson Álvarez or Soungoutou Magassa after both sought moves during the summer.

Magassa’s exile has caused disquiet. Not only is a young asset being devalued, a fragile defence is going without enough protection. West Ham have a lot of No 8s but they need a No 6. It is an area for Parker to address. He will also need to work out whether to stick with Mads Hermansen in goal, and the jury is out on summer signings.

One source questions whether West Ham targeted players equipped to handle the intensity of the Championship. Divine Mukasa, the 19-year-old midfielder, is talented but inconsistent. Manor Solomon and Edvin Austbø have flattered to deceive on the left wing. Joël Veltman, the 34-year-old Dutch right-back, has had fitness issues. Adama Boiro, a 24-year-old left‑back on loan from Athletic Bilbao, has not been seen since a cameo against Derby in early September. The three strikers signed by Nuno across two windows have toiled; Pablo Felipe, Taty Castellanos and Joël Piroe have five league goals between them this season.

Nils Koppen, the head of recruitment appointed in the summer, will have to be busy in January, but Parker will hope to have made his impact before the window opens. He is not known for expansive football but he is proven at this level. Parker won promotion with Bournemouth, Burnley and Fulham. The 46-year-old’s track record is clear and it helps that he has an association with West Ham.

Parker, whose all-action playing style made him a fan favourite, knows how difficult it can be in east London. He played in some pretty bad West Ham sides, experienced relegation in 2011 and arrives with the club in a tricky spot again.

It is not the moment to start worrying about Parker’s questionable record as a Premier League manager. What do West Ham do if they go up? Do they stick with Parker? Do they make an instant change? Well, how about they get promoted first? The rest can wait.

Pokémon Officially Gives Charizard A New Look For 30th Anniversary

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Pokémon has been celebrating its 30th anniversary all year long, and that shows no signs of stopping anytime soon. Even this month alone, there's a mysterious countdown to something on November 30. Previous months have seen the release of the 30th Celebration set in the Pokémon TCG, an Adidas collaboration, and the releases of FireRed and LeafGreen for Nintendo Switch and Switch 2. For most franchises, that might be it, but for Pokémon, that's barely scratching the surface.

And thanks to these collaborations, we're getting to see artists and designers put their own unique spins on beloved Pokémon. From plushes and shirts, to shoes and jewelry, the Pokémon of Gen 1 have been featured on a lot of merchandise throughout 2026. We've gotten Gengar gaming chairs, Mewtwo double-banded rings, and skateboard decks with fan-favorites like Eevee, Pikachu, and Gyarados.

As one of the most famous Pokémon from the Gen 1 era, Charizard has also gotten its fair share of merchandise for the 30th anniversary of the series, including its very own Adidas sneakers and a plush designed by Sean Wotherspoon. The ever-popular Fire-type was also featured on 30th Celebration cards, and is a core part of the new McDonald's Happy Meals.

Now, Charizard is getting another new look thanks to the "Evolve to Charizard Ring" by Enso. The ring features Charmander, Charmeleon, and Charizard, which are etched into the ring, but Charizard is the main star. The ring itself is made of medical-grade silicone, and it's currently up for sale in a number of adult sizes.

Available on the official Pokémon Center storefront, the "Evolve To Charizard Ring" by Enso is priced at $44.99. This is a ring for adults, with a sizing chart available; however, Size 13 is currently sold out. The other sizes are still available at the time of this writing. If you're a fan of the Gen 1 Fire-type starter evolutions, or know someone who is, then this could make a great gift ahead for the upcoming holiday season.

In addition to the Charizard ring, there's one featuring Gengar (also for $44.99), as well as a double-banded premium ring for Mewtwo for $129.99. The three rings from Enso are just some of the latest merchandise released to coincide with Pokémon's 30th anniversary. With only a couple of months left in 2026, these might also be some of the final items of the year.

For the most part, the 30th anniversary has been focused on the Gen 1 games. That makes sense. After all, those are the games that are actually turning 30 this year. That has meant older Pokémon have gotten the chance to shine, but it has also put an increasing spotlight on longtime favorites like Charizard, Pikachu, Gengar, Eevee, and Pikachu.

With the holidays approaching, Pokémon has also launched a holiday collection, where you can expect to find additional merchandise starring some of these fan-favorites from the Kanto region. In the meantime, it's been great to see all of these new designs released for Pokémon that many fans have adored for three decades now.

Dead End Job Season 2: Release Date, Story & Everything We Know

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Shealyn Scott is a Senior Writer at Screen Rant. She has been writing for the site since 2024, focused on network, reality, streaming, and classic television. A creative writer, journalist, and lover of the written word in all its forms, Shealyn enjoys deconstructing scenes from her favorite shows, using context clues and historical precedent to predict major plot points (which, due to her successful track record, has sparked rumors of clairvoyance). As an award-winning student journalist, Shealyn spent her college years advocating for the humanities while studying English Literature. Her love of storytelling propelled her to expand her degree with minors in Writing and History, believing life to be a mere collection of stories that can be framed in as many ways as a movie scene. As a Senior member of the TV Team, Shealyn treats the series she covers like books, analyzing every line, camera angle, and lighting choice. Thankfully, her personal mission statement lines up perfectly with Screen Rant: every creative work deserves just as much thought from the viewer as it received from its creator.

SPOILERS AHEAD for Dead-End Job season 1.After eight high-octane episodes of supernatural thrills and chills, the future of Dead-End Job remains unclear. Following Lee Jae-wook as Byeon Hyuk-jun, a heavily indebted man desperate for work, Netflix's latest K-drama sensation blends the morbidly whimsical life-or-death scenarios of Squid Game with deeply emotional subplots that cut through the terror with shockingly poignant twists. Without question, Dead-End Job is already one of Netflix's best new shows of 2026— but, sadly, its fate beyond season 1, episode 8 is still up in the air.

The horror elements of Dead-End Job tap into universal anxieties, providing some pithy commentary on capitalism while Hyuk-jun takes on a sinister amusement park, killer game show, and spine-chilling convenience store— each its own fully realized nightmare that preys on the protagonist's money stress with unrelenting chaos. Despite rave reviews and outspoken viewer support, however, it's possible that Netflix may squander the lingering potential of Dead-End Job. Of course, that's not to say there's no hope for the K-drama's possible renewal.

As of early November 2026, Netflix has not made a definitive decision regarding the future of Dead-End Job. Given the series only recently premiered on October 30, its spot in renewal/cancelation limbo is no cause for concern— at least not yet. Unfortunately, there are countless beloved Netflix K-dramas that never earned a second season, in spite of a breakout debut (Extraordinary Attorney Woo, Celebrity, Parsyte: The Grey, etc.).

Conversely, some Netflix original K-drama productions are planned far in advance, with renewals announced as soon as the first batch of episodes drop. Consequently, the supernatural drama may have been conceived as a limited series, but ratings and public opinion of season 1 may tip the scales and convince Netflix to green-light Dead-End Job season 2.

Although early reactions have been overwhelmingly positive, it's too soon to tell what will happen with Dead-End Job. To that end, Netflix may announce a renewal in days, attempt to generate buzz in early 2027, or leave the series hanging in production purgatory until it has decidedly fallen out of vogue. Alas, even if Netflix ordered Dead-End Job season 2 immediately, it likely wouldn't hit screens anytime soon.

The select group of K-dramas lucky enough to become multi-season titles in Netflix's extensive libraries have proven that renewal doesn't guarantee a quick turnaround. For instance, half a decade will have passed before All of Us Are Dead fans watch the season 2 premiere (expected in 2027), while Squid Game's second installment was released three years after its record-breaking debut in 2021.

Half a decade after the monumental start of Squid Game, Netflix's K-drama content continues to shape the streaming service's decade-defining rollout.

In stark contrast, The Trauma Code: Heroes on Call seasons 2 and 3 are in production simultaneously, and the anticipated release is hardly a demanding wait. As such, without any added variables, a K-drama's second season could take years. Yet, considering lead actor Lee Jae-wook is currently fulfilling his compulsory military service until November 2027, Dead-End Job season 2 couldn't possibly premiere until late 2028 or early 2029 at the earliest. Regardless, one major change could expedite the process: recasting Byeon Hyuk-jun.

Irrefutably, the globally trending horror K-drama can stand on its own as a concise, eight-episode binge. Nevertheless, Dead-End Job clearly set the stage for season 2 to follow Hyuk‑jun investigating the true nature of Spider Staffing Agency. Though the audience isn't privy to Hyuk-jun's answer to the offer of becoming Mr. S' (Lee Hee-joon) successor, the story can only go one of two ways: he accepts the position and tries to take it down from the inside, or he rejects it to uncover the larger, underlying conspiracy.

Likewise, the fates of Mr. S and Eum Yeon-joo (Go Min-si) are left purposely ambiguous; both may have died after being absorbed by the other world, or they could return to fight in a new, even more monstrous form. Either way, Dead-End Job season 2 would have plenty of questions to answer, not least of which pertaining to Yeon-joo's tragic backstory and Byeon Ji-yoon's (Kim Min-ha) role in the overarching plot. Whether a fresh face takes up the mantle as the main character or Netflix waits for Lee Jae-wook's discharge, however, Dead-End Job season 2 already feels like a must.

All 24 EFL League One stadiums ranked by how far they are from city centre

📅 2026-11-04  |  Read Source →

Every travelling football fan knows that journeys to grounds in England can range from simple to overly complicated, and that is certainly the case in League One.

Some supporters might travel down the night before or on the day depending on the distance, but the trip from the city centre to the stadium is one most home fans are used to.

But what are the longest and shortest distances from city centre to stadium? Here, FLW ranks every third-tier ground by their proximity to the city or town centre as the crow flies.

The furthest distance from the city centre in League One is from Oxford city centre to the Kassam Stadium, which is approximately a four-mile trip.

Oxford United's ground is situated in the south-east region of the city in Littlemore, just off the A4142.

MK Dons moved into Stadium MK in the summer of 2007, and it is one of the longest journeys from city centre to stadium in the division.

The ground is around three miles from the city centre in Denbigh North in the south-west part of Milton Keynes.

The biggest and one of the most historic grounds in the third tier, Hillsborough is roughly three miles from Sheffield city centre.

The journey on foot to Sheffield Wednesday's home takes just under an hour, but the easiest journey is via the Sheffield Supertram.

Formerly known as the Madejski Stadium, the Select Car Leasing Stadium is located around two miles from the centre of Reading.

From Reading town centre, you can take a bus towards Tilehurst, with the ground served by several routes.

Also around two miles from High Wycombe town centre is Adams Park, the home of Wycombe Wanderers.

From High Wycombe town centre, the ground is accessible by bus, with services stopping close to the stadium.

One of several London clubs in the division, Bromley is situated on the edge of South East London and is the largest borough in the capital by area.

From Bromley town centre, you can take a bus towards Hayes Lane, with the journey at around 1.9 miles.

A slightly shorter journey from the city centre to the ground, the trip to Plymouth Argyle's ground, Home Park, is roughly 1.8 miles.

Home Park sits in the Milehouse area of Plymouth, with regular buses from the city centre making it a simple trip to the stadium.

Situated a mile and a half from Burton town centre, you can find the home of Burton Albion, the Pirelli Stadium.

From Burton town centre, you can hop on a bus heading towards Eton Park before making the final short walk to the ground.

Cambridge United's ground, the Cledara Abbey Stadium, is situated approximately 1.4 miles away from the city centre.

Newmarket Road leads you east from the city centre towards the Abbey Stadium, with regular buses making the journey particularly simple.

At around 1.3 miles from the city centre, the journey to Doncaster Rovers' home ranks as the 14th longest journey on the list.

The Club Doncaster Sports Village is within walking distance of central Doncaster, although local buses offer an easier alternative for those who would rather save their legs.
